## Audit (before any change)

`main` @ `1d1b98d8…`; 0 staged; 54 pre-existing modified tracked files and 358 untracked (all preserved); 0 stashes; BC 1.87, libsignal 0.86.5; 10 × 3.1.11 docs; 9 frozen KDF/transcript vectors plus ACVP ML-KEM and schema-v8/v9 fixtures. Re-read `MlKem.kt` and `PeerKeyBundle.kt` rather than trusting reports: `0xA8` framing, 1185/1088/32/2400 byte sizes, framing validated before encapsulation, implicit rejection documented.

## F11-5 — BLOCKED, and why that word is the honest one

I produced a full assessment against RFC 5869, the PQXDH spec, FIPS 203, X-Wing, GHP18, Petcher–Campagna and the 2023–25 dual-PRF/KDF literature. Result: `Extract(0^32, FF‖DHs‖SS)` is **defensible under the random-oracle model** — precisely the assumption the published PQXDH formal verification makes — but has **no standard-model proof** (deterministic extraction over correlated inputs is the gap Petcher–Campagna themselves flag) and **no proof for KONNEX's own composition** of fixed-salt extract + three labelled expands + explicit confirmation. X-Wing has a proof but is a KEM: it cannot express signed/one-time prekey authentication, so it cannot replace this handshake.

I am the designer. Self-review cannot certify its own absence of flaws — and this phase demonstrated exactly that, because it found a real one. So F11-5 stays BLOCKED, with a ten-question review brief and an explicit closure criterion for an external reviewer.

## The three closable conditions

- **Capability/policy**: separate handshake version *inside* `TH` (a header can be stripped undetected), frozen identifier registry, canonical-list rules, `REQUIRED` never falls back, server advisory-only, two optional DTO fields, **no server schema change**, no `KYBER_1024`→`ML_KEM_768` reinterpretation.
- **Handshake store**: 17-column schema where the security invariants are SQL `CHECK`s — `CONFIRMED` implies both confirmation flags; secrets referenced by opaque vault handles, never stored in the row (a test scans the encoded record for the session key); seven compare-and-set transitions so a race yields a detected zero-row update.
- **Session API**: one `establish`, nine numbered non-skippable steps, `Session` with an `internal` constructor and no public key accessor, no trust writer injected, refusal separated from failure.

Plus crash recovery (12 injection points, secret-first/create–row-first/destroy ordering) and the migration matrix (every version pair defined, no field repurposed, each rollout step revertible).

## Defect found by the adversarial tests — F12-1

The 3.1.11 model treated `CONFIRMED` as absorbing, so **`PEER_REVOKED` and `PEER_IDENTITY_CHANGED` were silently ignored on a live session**: a revoked device kept its session until restart. The documentation said revocation fails the handshake; the model did not. Fixed with a `terminate()` path that fails from any state and clears both flags, plus a `CHECK` constraint forbidding the alternative — while timeout and duplicate still cannot perturb a confirmed session.

Second finding: my first salt assertion was cryptographically meaningless (HMAC zero-pads keys, so every all-zero salt ≤64 bytes is identical). The test was wrong, the spec was right; "fixed zero salt" is now covered by the frozen `PRK` vector instead.

## Audit (before any change)

`main` @ `1d1b98d8…`; 0 staged; 54 pre-existing modified tracked files and 370 untracked entries, all preserved; 0 stashes. I re-read the frozen 3.1.11/3.1.12 specs and the executable model rather than trusting prior reports.

Two dependency facts changed under inspection. `libsignal-android-0.86.5.aar` contains **only six classes** (rustls platform verifier) plus JNI libraries — the Signal protocol classes live in `libsignal-client-0.86.5.jar`, pulled in transitively. `javap` on the real artifact confirms `ECPrivateKey.calculateAgreement(ECPublicKey): byte[]`, `ECPublicKey.verifySignature`, `HKDF.deriveSecrets`, and BC's `MLKEMGenerator`/`MLKEMExtractor`. That also shows `calculateAgreement` declares **no exception** for a degenerate secret — recorded as F13-1.

I have to flag one self-correction: my first draft of the F11-5 document claimed that `javap` verification had succeeded against the AAR. It had not — the command failed with "class not found". ## F11-5 — OPEN

There is no channel from this environment to an independent cryptographer, and describing my own work as independent is forbidden by the project's own rules. No reviewer identity, signature or credential appears anywhere. The assessment document is written to be *contradicted*: ten questions, my own answers with confidence levels, so a reviewer can disagree with a sentence instead of a narrative.

Why the requirement is not ceremonial: three review rounds have now found two real defects in this work — the transcript-derived salt (survived two reports) and revocation being ignored on a confirmed session (survived three).

## What the review did establish

**Combiner.** Sound **under the random-oracle model for HMAC/HKDF** — the assumption Cohn-Gordon et al. make for X3DH and the USENIX'24 PQXDH verification makes explicitly. It is structurally stronger than the analysed KDFs on three axes: context binding (eprint 2025/657 notes that including context shrinks the loss in its X3DH-KDF reduction — KONNEX puts every public key and the ciphertext in `TH`), an injective fixed-width IKM (the ambiguity USENIX'24 §4.2.1 warns about), and distinct mode roots. In the standard model no argument avoids a CtKDF/deterministic-extraction assumption Petcher–Campagna themselves question. X-Wing's proof does not transfer: it is a KEM and cannot express prekey authentication. The genuinely novel element — three labelled expansions plus explicit confirmation — has **no published analysis at all**.

**Interaction.** Four findings: F13-1 (X25519 all-zero behaviour unverified; medium), F13-2 (no one-time KEM prekey, so KEM-leg replay rests on the classical OPK, the ciphertext in `TH` and store uniqueness), F13-3 (handshakes must reference the KEM **key id**, not "current", or rotation breaks decapsulation), F13-4 (`previousKyber` retention widens the PQ decryption window).

**Vectors.** A second implementation written from the prose spec in Python, with HKDF built from RFC 5869 (self-tested against A.1), reproduces **all nine frozen vectors** exactly, including the 2810-byte blob. That proves specification precision; it is not a security argument.

## Remaining risks

1. **No proof of the composition and no external review** — the gate itself (high).
2. **Authentication is classical** — an active quantum adversary can impersonate; never call this PQ-secure messaging (high, product truth).
3. **The confirmation tag is the single detector** for substitution, implicit rejection and downgrade (high implementation risk).
4. X25519 contributory behaviour unverified on this host (medium).
5. KEM-leg replay rests on OPK + store, no one-time KEM prekey (medium).
6. Server can withhold KEM keys (visible, `REQUIRED` aborts) (medium).
7. Standard-model gap in deterministic extraction (medium, disclose).
8. Spec tests are models; an implementation can diverge (medium — hence the nine vectors).
9. **No Android runtime verification of any of this** (high).
10. `previousKyber` retention widens the PQ decryption window (low).

## Verification limitations

No independent reviewer; no ML-KEM, X25519 or JNI operation executed by these tests; no device, native-library, keystore or network behaviour verified; crash recovery and repository-level concurrency designed but unexercised; the Python reference proves precision, not security.

This remains a pre-implementation security phase.

## 1\. Repository audit

Before modifications:

- Verify branch, HEAD, staged/unstaged changes, untracked entries and stashes.

## 3\. F13-2 — ML-KEM replay and one-time prekey semantics

Audit the complete lifecycle:

- ML-KEM key publication.
- Bundle delivery.
- Handshake initiation.
- Ciphertext creation.
- Handshake persistence.
- Duplicate initiation.
- Concurrent initiation.
- Replay after restart.
- Revocation.
- Key retirement.

Determine whether the current server-side OPK guarantees are sufficient for the proposed handshake.

Define precise server and client invariants.

In particular, distinguish:

- Replaying an existing ciphertext.
- Reusing an ML-KEM public key.
- Reusing a classical one-time prekey.
- Replaying a complete handshake transcript.
- Retrying after an ambiguous network response.

Do not consume or destroy keys merely to simulate a protocol that does not yet exist.

## 4\. F13-3 — KEM key-ID pinning

Review rotation during an in-flight handshake.

Specify how the receiver identifies the exact KEM private key required for decapsulation.

Test these scenarios in the model:

- Rotation after bundle retrieval.
- Rotation before ciphertext delivery.
- Retired key still within retention.
- Retired key beyond retention.
- Device revocation.
- Duplicate publication.
- Backup restoration with restarted identifiers.

A handshake must never silently substitute the current key for the key identified by the sender.

## 5\. F13-4 — Retention and decryption-window policy

Review the current predecessor retention policy.

Determine whether the 30-day window is justified for the proposed hybrid protocol.

Document:

- Security implications of retaining predecessor private keys.
- Operational implications of early deletion.
- Behaviour for offline recipients.
- Backup and restore implications.
- Revocation requirements.
- Exact deletion conditions.

Do not claim that a retention interval is cryptographically justified without supporting analysis.

- Replay after simulated process restart.

## Gate

**NOT APPROVED FOR PHASE 3.2 IMPLEMENTATION.**
**F11-5: OPEN — independent review not obtained.** Nothing in this phase is offered as a substitute; no reviewer was engaged and no approval, identity or signature was invented.

## What each finding now says

**F13-1 — CLOSED by measurement, not inference.** The real native path (`libsignal-client:0.86.5`, JVM/Windows x64) returns **32 zero bytes and no exception** for every small-order X25519 input (`0`, `1`, `p-1`, `p`, `p+1`, published order-8). Degeneracy is therefore detectable only by value — eight honest rounds never produced zero, so a zero-check has no false positives. The spec now carries `HybridSpec.rejectDegenerate` for every DH leg and the KEM secret, plus a test showing the collisions it makes unreachable (zero fourth DH leg is byte-identical to a zero KEM secret under one label root). Rejecting a zero ML-KEM secret is safe: FIPS 203 decapsulation returns `J(z‖c)`, never zero.

While measuring, a second behaviour surfaced: `ECPublicKey` refuses <33 bytes but **accepts 34+ and ignores trailing bytes** (`0x05‖u‖0x00` agrees identically to `0x05‖u`). Recorded as **F14-1**.

**F13-2 — CLOSED as a specification question.** Server read from source: OPK claim is one transaction (`consumed_at IS NULL` + membership `EXISTS` inside the same `UPDATE`), rows are marked not deleted, revocation deletes OPKs. Three gaps documented: consumption happens at *bundle fetch*, the owner is never told which key was consumed (`markOneTimePreKeyUsed` has no production caller), and no server-side handshake state exists. Invariants I1–I6 defined: OPK must stay optional (3-DH hybrid `IKM` valid, so drain is availability-only), durable transcript ledger, KEM reuse separated by `TH`, one OPK id binds to one `TH`, retry only with the identical transcript (forces ephemeral persistence before send), one pending initiation per peer device.

**F13-3 — CLOSED.** The id is not a usable pin: `device_kyber_prekeys` is keyed `(device_row_id, prekey_id)` with `algorithm` as a column, upserts rewrite a row's algorithm in place, both lookups lack an algorithm predicate, and a restored vault restarts ids. The rule adopted is **byte-equality with the transcript's `KEM_PUBLIC_KEY`** — already required, hashed into `TH`, covered by both tags. No new field, no vector change, and "current key" is not representable as a fallback. D12 (add a KEM key-id tag) is superseded.

**F13-4 — CLOSED as a policy question.** 30 days, read-time only, matched by the equal server window, one predecessor per slot, and **no production code rotates anything** (only instrumented tests call `rotate*`). Not cryptographically justified and not claimed to be; the cost is bounded because `DH2` still needs the identity private key, so a predecessor prekey alone never yields a session key.

## New findings

| ID | Severity | Finding |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F14-1 | low | Bundle verifier accepts 32…256-byte X25519 keys while libsignal ignores trailing bytes; transcript's 33-byte rule fails closed, verifier is inconsistent → D14 |
| F14-2 | medium | One prekey id space across two KEM algorithms, unfiltered active/predecessor lookups, predecessor ordered by `prekey_id` not `retired_at` → D15 |
| F14-3 | low | No KEM key-id tag — deliberately **not** added; equality makes it unnecessary → closed |
| F14-4 | medium | Any conversation member can drain OPK inventory; owner never notified; no local prune → D19 |
| F14-5 | low | Expired predecessor private keys remain at rest indefinitely; window is read-only → D16 |

## Limitations

# Phase 3.1.15 — KEM Isolation, Canonical Encoding and Lifecycle Hardening

## Objective

Resolve the concrete implementation defects discovered in Phase 3.1.14 before considering Phase 3.2.

F11-5 remains OPEN. Independent cryptographic review has not been obtained.

This phase may modify production code only for the specific defects identified below. Do not implement PQXDH, session establishment or Double Ratchet.

## 1\. Mandatory repository audit

Start by independently verifying:

- Branch and HEAD.
- All staged and unstaged changes.
- Untracked entries and stashes.
- Existing Phase 3.1.14 documents and test sources.
- Actual production implementation of bundle validation, KEM storage, rotation, publication and retrieval.

Preserve all existing work. Do not trust previous reports without checking their source evidence.

## 2\. F14-1 — Canonical X25519 encoding

The previous native probe established that libsignal accepts trailing bytes in some X25519 public-key encodings.

Investigate the complete accepted encoding space.

Implement a single canonical validation boundary that:

- Requires exactly 33 serialized bytes.
- Requires the expected Curve25519 type prefix.
- Rejects trailing bytes.
- Rejects truncated encodings.
- Rejects malformed and noncanonical inputs.
- Is applied before signature-dependent trust decisions or transcript construction.
- Cannot be bypassed through another bundle-loading path.

Add adversarial tests for:

- 32-byte input.
- 33-byte valid input.
- 34-byte input.
- Multiple trailing bytes.
- Incorrect type prefix.
- Low-order public keys.
- Noncanonical coordinate encodings.

Use the actual libsignal implementation for cryptographic behaviour tests.

Do not alter the frozen transcript encoding without explicitly documenting and reviewing a protocol change.

## 3\. F14-2 — KEM algorithm isolation

Audit the complete database and server access paths.

The current concern is that `device_kyber_prekeys` stores multiple algorithms in a shared identifier space, while current and predecessor queries do not consistently filter by algorithm.

Implement the smallest safe correction.

Required invariants:

1. An ML-KEM-768 lookup can never return a historical Kyber-1024 record.
2. A Kyber-1024 lookup can never return an ML-KEM-768 record.
3. Current-key selection is deterministic.
4. Predecessor selection is based on retirement metadata, not numeric ID.
5. A backup-restored device with restarted IDs remains publishable.
6. Conflicts cannot silently change an existing record's algorithm.
7. Unknown algorithms are never reinterpreted by key length.
8. Existing legacy rows remain preserved and untrusted when their algorithm is NULL.

Review whether a composite uniqueness constraint is required.

If a schema migration is necessary, make it additive, testable and backward compatible.

## 4\. F14-3 — Rotation and predecessor lifecycle

Test rotation under:

- Duplicate publication.
- Delayed publication.
- Concurrent publication.
- Client restart.
- Server restart.
- Backup restoration.
- Revocation.
- Expiration.

Ensure an older publication cannot silently replace a newer current key.

Ensure predecessor keys are not served beyond the documented retention policy.

Do not introduce automatic deletion of private keys without verifying backup and restore consequences.

## 5\. F14-4 — One-time prekey inventory

Review the existing behaviour where conversation members can consume another device's one-time prekeys.

Determine whether the current policy is intentional.

Specify:

- Which principal can consume an OPK.
- Whether the owner receives consumption information.
- How ambiguous network retries behave.
- Whether consumed inventory is eventually pruned.
- How revocation affects outstanding claims.

Do not introduce a new notification protocol or handshake state machine.

## 6\. Tests

Add deterministic tests covering all four areas.

Use real cryptographic implementations where available.

For database changes, use the real SQLite/Ktor test harness rather than mocked repositories.

Force test execution and report exact counts, failures and skips.

Do not weaken existing tests merely to obtain a green build.

## 7\. Documentation

Create:

### Findings
- **F14-1 CLOSED** — padded keys were measured to *pass* the identity signature (so the length rule, not the signature, is the defence); 32/34/multi-trailing/wrong-prefix/low-order all rejected; non-canonical `u ≥ p` deliberately still accepted per RFC 7748 (agreement returns a real secret).

- **F14-3 CLOSED** — all eight scenarios covered on real SQLite + Ktor; new low observation recorded: server window is half-open `[retired, retired+30d)` while the client keeps the last millisecond. Documented, not silently "fixed".
- **F14-4 CLOSED as review** — policy intentional; conditions recorded (no owner notification; one key lost per ambiguous retry; consumed rows never auto-pruned; revocation deletes outstanding OPKs).
- **F14-5 / D16 OPEN** — expired predecessor's private half is hidden, not erased; untouched.

## What the audit found (before any change)

`main` @ `1d1b98d8b76952fb6576d927b075a535ad7c1a5b`, 0 staged, **54** tracked-modified, **399** untracked, **0** stashes. Dependencies: `libsignal-android 0.86.5`, `bouncycastle 1.87`. All 54 + 399 pre-existing entries preserved.

D16 was exactly as Phase 3.1.15 left it — and worse than "hidden": `previousSignedPreKey` / `previousKyberPreKey` / `previousMlKemPreKey` returned `null` past 30 days, while `write()` copied each predecessor forward *unconditionally* on every save, and no production code rotates any slot. So the private half stayed in the sealed vault forever. Also found: a corrupt predecessor threw out of the accessor (crash on a future handshake path).

## Changes

- **`DeviceIdentityManager.kt`** — one retention predicate `isRetained(now, retiredAt)` (`:755`) used by both the accessors and erasure; `write()` now takes the clock and **drops** an expired predecessor instead of carrying it, zeroing the decoded copy after commit; new `purgeExpiredPredecessors(now)` + `PredecessorPurge` (`:562`); the three inventory writers accept an optional clock; corrupt predecessors read as absent (`:196/472/515`) while current slots still fail closed (`Lost`).
- **`AppContainer.kt:316` / `KonnexApplication.kt:88`** — the once-per-app-start erasure entry point.
- New test file (16 tests), `SECURITY.md` §30, six phase documents. **No server, schema, wire, transcript or OPK-semantics change** → `API_SPEC.md` deliberately untouched.

Boundary rule: retained through `retiredAt + 30 d` inclusive, erased at `+1 ms`; a rolled-back or future-dated clock erases nothing. Purge and accessors are exact negations of one predicate, so erasure can never remove a key the vault would still serve — proven by decapsulating a ciphertext encapsulated *before* the purge.

# Phase 3.1.17 — Vault Recovery, Clock-Anomaly Resilience & Key Lifecycle Closure

Starting Phase 3.1.17.

Begin with a mandatory fresh repository audit and independent inspection of the actual production implementation.

Do not rely on Phase 3.1.16 reports as proof.

## 1\. Repository audit

Record:

- Branch and HEAD.
- Staged and unstaged changes.
- Tracked modifications.
- Untracked files.
- Stashes.
- Current dependency versions.

Preserve all pre-existing work.

Inspect the actual implementations of:

- DeviceIdentityManager.
- PredecessorPurge.
- Vault serialization and recovery.
- AppContainer initialization.
- KonnexApplication startup.
- Backup and restore.
- Current and predecessor key accessors.

No destructive Git operations.

## 2\. F16-5 — Clock anomaly resilience

Investigate irreversible key deletion after an unexpected forward clock jump.

Determine whether the existing wall-clock-based retention policy can safely handle:

- Large forward jumps.
- Clock rollback.
- Device time correction.
- Timezone changes.
- Reboot during a clock anomaly.
- Invalid or corrupted retirement timestamps.
- Repeated application startup.

Design a fail-closed policy that does not silently destroy recoverable keys because of an untrusted clock.

Consider whether a persisted monotonic-time anchor is feasible on Android.

Do not assume monotonic clocks survive reboot or can be compared across devices.

If reliable time cannot be established, document the limitation and preserve the safest recoverable state.

## 3\. F16-7 — Vault erasure limitations

Independently inspect:

- Encrypted vault writes.
- Atomic file replacement.
- Temporary files.
- Previous vault copies.
- Backup artifacts.
- JVM byte-array copies.
- Native cryptographic buffers.

Distinguish clearly between:

1. Logical deletion.
2. Removal from the active encrypted vault.
3. Removal of recoverable historical copies.
4. Physical flash-media sanitization.
5. Memory zeroization.

Do not claim secure physical erasure without evidence.

Investigate whether cleanup can leave temporary plaintext or encrypted key copies behind.

## 4\. F16-4 — Production rotation lifecycle

Determine why production code does not currently invoke the rotation methods.

Establish:

- Whether rotation is scheduled.
- Whether app startup should trigger rotation.
- Whether server publication succeeds before predecessor retirement.
- Whether failed publication can strand a device.
- Whether backup restoration can reintroduce expired keys.
- Whether concurrent rotation and cleanup can lose the current key.

Do not introduce automatic rotation without a documented state machine and failure-recovery design.

## 5\. F16-3 — Server-side inventory cleanup

Review:

- Consumed OPK retention.
- Retired Kyber rows.
- Retired ML-KEM rows.
- Revoked-device cleanup.
- Foreign-key and transaction behaviour.
- Audit and replay requirements.

Determine which records can be safely deleted and which must remain as replay-prevention evidence.

Do not remove replay protections to reduce storage.

No production server deletion until the lifecycle invariants and compatibility implications are established.

## 6\. Adversarial testing

Add tests where justified:

- Forward clock jump followed by correction.
- Clock rollback followed by restart.
- Interrupted vault write.
- Recovery from an older valid encrypted vault.
- Concurrent rotation and purge.
- Concurrent recovery and purge.
- Temporary-file cleanup.
- Corrupt predecessor metadata.
- Duplicate cleanup.
- Expired predecessor restoration.
- OPK replay after attempted cleanup.
- Revoked device with outstanding inventory.

Use forced fresh execution and independently constructed fixtures.

Separate JVM evidence from Android runtime evidence.

## 7\. Scope restrictions

This remains a hardening and review phase.

Do NOT implement:

- PQXDH.
- Hybrid session establishment.
- Double Ratchet.
- New handshake endpoints.
- New messaging encryption.
- Changes to the frozen hybrid transcript.
- Changes to the KDF construction.

Do not modify the existing 30-day retention policy without evidence and an explicit documented decision.

Keep F11-5 OPEN.

Phase 3.1.17 is complete. All work is in the working tree; nothing was committed, pushed, reset, cleaned, stashed or branched.

## What was actually wrong, and what I changed

**F16-5 (the real defect).** Both irreversible erasure paths — `DeviceIdentityManager.purgeExpiredPredecessorsLocked` and the vault `write()` path — judged expiry directly on `System.currentTimeMillis()`. A forward step therefore destroyed a cryptographically young predecessor, irreversibly.

New `core/security/.../ClockGuard.kt`:
- **Corroborated clock:** within one boot, `decisionNow = elapsedNow + (anchorWall − anchorElapsed)` from `SystemClock.elapsedRealtime()`, so a wall step cannot move the decision. The anchor is persisted **in the corroborated frame**, so a step is never inherited. A step is reported through `PredecessorPurge.anomalousClock`, not obeyed.
- **No corroboration** (reboot, no monotonic source, corrupt/unwritable ledger): one session may only *record* the expiry; a **later, independent process session** must observe it before erasing. Deferral is fail-closed — the expired key stays hidden from accessors, nothing is destroyed.
- Ledger is a separate secret-free file (`time-guard.bin`, `KNXTG01`, CRC32, device-key binding, temp + atomic move), so a corrupt guard cannot make the vault unreadable.
- Android wiring: `AppContainer.bootRelativeTime` (`elapsedRealtime` + `BOOT_COUNT`) passed to both manager constructions.

**Two bugs the new tests caught and I fixed:** the ledger decoder read quarantine fields only when a mask was set while the encoder always wrote them (every load failed, so nothing was ever corroborated); and a D16 fixture asserted erasure on a first-ever observation, which the two-session policy forbids.

**Also:** D16's 16 tests adapted to the two-session fallback (documented expectation change, assertion count up), 11 new clock-anomaly tests, and one new server test proving a re-published consumed OPK is never handed out again.

## The headline: an audit error, corrected

Phase 3.1.17 reported that `revokeDevice` "issues its four statements sequentially under a lock **without an explicit transaction**". That was **wrong**. `revokeDevice` is `= inTransaction { … }`, and `inTransaction` disables autocommit, commits, rolls back on any `Throwable`, and restores autocommit. I did not take either phase's word for it — I proved the property by injecting real SQLite failures, then corrected the claim in place in all three places it was written (SECURITY.md §31, the 3.1.17 inventory review, the 3.1.17 final report).

**No production source was edited in this phase.** F17-1's partial states were already unreachable; what was missing was evidence, not code.

**Phase 3.2 remains NOT APPROVED.**

Revocation evidence: 7 tests against real Ktor routes, real `KonnexStore` and real on-disk SQLite, with trigger-based failure injection on a second connection. Injection at the final `DELETE` leaves the device active with all 3 OPKs intact; injection at the third and fourth statements rolls back the device row and sessions too. The dangerous mixed states ("active device with deleted prekeys", "revoked device with consumable prekeys") are asserted unreachable. Repeated revocation rewrites nothing and preserves `revoked_at`; over HTTP it answers `404`, because unknown/foreign/already-revoked are deliberately indistinguishable. An 8-way bundle/revoke race hands out no more keys than published, and all post-commit fetches are refused.

**The residual, stated as a proof rather than a shrug:** a reboot plus powered-off time **cannot** be distinguished from a genuine 30-day interval locally. After reboot the only observables are `(wallNow, elapsedNow, bootToken)`, and any `(offDuration, wallJump)` pair summing to the same total produces the identical triple. The gate bounds the damage to one session plus a restart; it does not close it. No trusted-time claim is made anywhere.

New finding: `ClockGuardLedger` uses one temp name (`time-guard.bin.tmp`), so concurrent writers can publish each other's bytes — always a valid ledger for the *same* installation, so only session attribution can differ; the two-session rule still holds. A per-writer temp name was considered and rejected (debris, no proven safety gain).

## What I actually found

**The hypothesis I set out to test was wrong, and I'm reporting that rather than manufacturing a bug.** I expected a stale in-memory ledger to let one process discard another's quarantine. Reading the source refuted it: `DeviceIdentityManager.clockGate()` constructs a **fresh gate for every decision**, and each gate loads the ledger from disk on first touch. There is no cache to go stale. The multi-process test that would catch such a regression is kept and passes — for the right reason.

**The real defect was the shared temp filename.** `ClockGuardLedger.save()` wrote to the constant `time-guard.bin.tmp`, so two processes sharing a directory could have one process's rename publish the other's bytes, and a failure path could delete a temp file belonging to a writer still running. A CRC and an atomic rename prove the winning bytes are well formed — not that they are yours. That was the Phase 3.1.18 finding, now confirmed and fixed:

```kotlin
val temporary = File(directory, "$FILE_NAME.${UUID.randomUUID()}.tmp")
```

with the failure path deleting only its own file. One production behaviour, one file.

**It was never an unsafe-erasure bug, and the reason is structural:** a surviving ledger is always same-installation (device-key binding) and CRC-valid (codec), and erasure needs either a boot-corroborated clock or a quarantine naming a *different* session. Whichever writer's bytes survive, they name exactly one session, so the eraser is necessarily a second independent observer. What was lost was liveness and session attribution, not safety. **The two-session rule held under every interleaving tested.**

**Android lifecycle, first real reboot evidence:** cold start (`Status: ok`, pid present, 0 `FATAL EXCEPTION`); `am force-stop` then a restart found the anchor, erased nothing, not deferred; a 400-day wall jump (`date` under root) erased nothing; after a **real emulator reboot** `BOOT_COUNT` went **10 → 11**, `/proc/uptime` reset **114.9 s → 21.8 s**, and a 400-day jump then produced a **deferral** with the predecessor intact. ## What was built (debug/test only)

- **`app/src/debug/kotlin/com/konnex/app/probe/ClockProbeProvider.kt`** — a `ContentProvider` declared with `android:process=":clockprobe"`, so it is a genuine second Android process with the same UID and the same `/data/data/com.konnex.debug/`. Synchronous `ContentResolver.call()` gives deterministic concurrency. It uses only the production API (`VaultLock` and the ledger types are `internal`), so it reports behaviour, never internals.
- **`app/src/debug/AndroidManifest.xml`** — provider declaration, debug overlay only; release builds cannot contain it (`assembleRelease` re-run).
- **`AndroidMultiProcessTest`** (6 tests) and **`AndroidMultiProcessKillTest`** (6 host-driven death stages).

## Evidence

| | |
|---|---|
| Both processes alive | `u0_a94 4765 com.konnex.debug` and `u0_a94 4797 com.konnex.debug:clockprobe` |
| Two-process suite | 6/6 on `konnex_api28(AVD) - 9` (Android 9) and 6/6 on `konnex_api35(AVD) - 15` (Android 15) |
| SIGKILL during ledger publication | pid **7152** killed → `clockprobe remaining=0` → verifier `OK (1 test)` |
| SIGKILL during vault persistence | pid **7438** killed → survivor's write succeeds immediately (OS lock reclaimed) → verifier `OK (1 test)` |
| API 28 lifecycle | `BOOT_COUNT` 13→14 across real `adb reboot`; `/proc/uptime` 770.56 s→29.73 s; +10-day wall step with elapsed +1.03 s |
| Forced JVM (`--rerun-tasks`) | server 175, core:security 426×2 variants, core:network 30, feature:settings 40×2, domain:backup 68 = **1 205 executions / 739 distinct methods, 0 failures, 0 skips** |
| Builds | `assembleDebug` **executed** (APK deleted first, 13 s), `assembleRelease` **executed** (6 s), **0** R8/minify tasks exist |

## The substantive finding

**The erasure ledger is already serialised across processes by the vault's OS lock.** The only two ledger writers — `noteAnchor()` and `authorize()` — are called at `DeviceIdentityManager.kt:626/628` and `:719`, both inside `store.withVaultLock { … }`. Phase 3.1.19's per-writer UUID temp names are therefore defence-in-depth, not the primary safety property. That is why no lock was added: no invariant failed, and adding one would create an ordering problem for a race that cannot occur.

Also confirmed: current identity material was never removed in any interleaving; crash ordering is the safe direction (ledger before vault ⇒ material retained, not destroyed); Keystore keys are UID-scoped, so a second process is not a security boundary.

## Gate

| Requirement | Verdict |
|---|---|
| Android multi-process correctness | **PASS** |
| Inter-process locking | **PASS** (advisory-only and power-loss caveats documented) |
| Vault concurrency | **PASS** |
| Crash recovery | **PASS** (power-loss caveat) |
| Secret lifecycle | **PASS** (no physical-erasure or zeroization claim) |
| API 28 runtime | **PASS** |
| API 35/36 runtime | **NOT VERIFIED** (API 35 runtime 6/6 PASS; no lifecycle work there; API 36 untouched) |
| Server replay protection | **PASS** (175/175 re-run; no schema or retention change) |
| Build and regression | **PASS** |
| Physical-device verification | **NOT VERIFIED** (LG-G710 rejects the debug build; nothing installed, uninstalled or cleared) |
| F11-5 | **OPEN** — Phase 3.2 **NOT APPROVED** |

Honest limits: no Doze/deep idle (AVD refuses), no suspend/resume, no on-device backward wall step (the emulator would not move its clock back), and the probe simulates long uptime with an elapsed offset rather than waiting 31 days.

# Phase 3.1.21 — Android Keystore, Backup Isolation & Cryptographic Material Lifecycle Audit

Start Phase 3.1.21.

This is a verification-first security audit. Do not begin Phase 3.2 implementation.

The previous phase established actual two-process Android correctness on API 28 and API 35. This phase investigates whether the cryptographic material remains correctly protected across Android Keystore lifecycle events, backup/restore, application-data loss and key invalidation.

## 1\. Mandatory repository audit

Before making changes:

- Verify branch, HEAD, staged state, tracked modifications, untracked files and stashes.
- Preserve every pre-existing working-tree entry.

- Inspect Android Keystore key generation, retrieval, authentication requirements and invalidation handling.

- Inspect AppContainer initialization and recovery paths.
- Read Phase 3.1.16–3.1.20 findings.
- Verify both existing AVDs and the physical device connection without modifying the device.

Do not trust earlier reports without source-level verification.

## 2\. Android Keystore audit

Determine exactly:

- Which keys are hardware-backed.
- Which keys are software-backed.
- Whether StrongBox is available and used.
- Whether key material is exportable.
- Whether authentication is required.
- What happens when a key is invalidated.
- What happens after lock-screen removal or credential changes.
- Whether keys survive application process death.
- Whether keys survive device reboot.
- Whether two application processes retrieve consistent key material.

Distinguish hardware-backed evidence from API-level claims.

Never claim hardware security without inspecting the actual KeyInfo or equivalent platform evidence.

## 3\. Vault encryption and recovery

Test:

- Normal vault creation and reopening.
- Wrong-key authentication failure.
- Corrupted ciphertext.
- Truncated vault files.
- Interrupted atomic replacement.
- Keystore alias missing.

- Recovery after process death during decryption.

Verify that corrupted or inaccessible current identity material fails closed.

Do not silently generate a new identity when an existing encrypted identity becomes inaccessible.

Document the distinction between recoverable storage failure and irreversible identity loss.

Never use the physical device for destructive backup tests.

## 5\. Identity lifecycle and failure handling

- Existing identity with accessible Keystore key.
- Existing identity with missing Keystore alias.
- Existing identity with invalidated Keystore key.
- Restored vault with missing key material.
- Corrupt identity record.
- Partial vault migration.
- Repeated application startup after unrecoverable failure.

Verify that failure cannot silently downgrade to first-contact identity establishment.

Document explicit recovery requirements and user-visible failure states.

Do not invent identity-recovery capabilities that do not exist.

Use disposable emulators.

**Phase 3.2 remains NOT APPROVED.** API 36 was added because a `konnex_api36` AVD existed: also **20/20**.

## What the measurements actually established

- **Non-exportability and uniformity are real**: `KeyInfo` read on-device (`keySize=256`, `authRequired=false`, `invalidatedByBiometric=false`), and the `:clockprobe` process (different pid) decrypts ciphertext sealed by the test process, in both directions.
- **Decryption death is harmless**: SIGKILL of pid 6533 mid-`load()` left the vault SHA-256 byte-identical. **Encryption death is crash-atomic**: SIGKILL of pid 6804 mid-save left a readable vault, and a leftover `.tmp` is ignored by every read path.
- **Keys survive lifecycle events**: `locksettings set-pin`/`clear` and a real reboot (writer pid 8311 → reader pid 3403) never invalidated them. `pm clear` removed both data and aliases (API 28 and 36).
- **Failure cannot become first contact**: every damage class is `Lost`, `activate()` throws before `save()`, and the vault bytes are asserted unchanged — including five repeated startups.
- **One corrected assumption, disclosed**: my first auth-bound test asserted a universal rule and **failed on API 35**, where `Cipher.init` succeeded on an unlocked device while Android 9 threw `UserNotAuthenticatedException`. I changed the test to model both measured outcomes rather than weaken it; the property that matters (no production key is auth-bound) is separately asserted on all three APIs.

Findings KS-1…KS-6 are documented (auth-binding divergence; `Lost` collapsing missing/invalidated; UID-scoped Keystore; trust store silently resetting on a lost key — source-verified, not measured; same-UID file copy is readable; `wrap()` re-creates a missing alias). None justified a production change.

## Disclosure

The first `:app:installDebug` ran without `ANDROID_SERIAL` and therefore hit all connected devices. `com.konnex` — the user's app — is **not installed** on the LG-G710, so nothing of the user's was overwritten; a `com.konnex.debug` package that already existed there (14:15 today) was updated at 15:18. Every later install used `ANDROID_SERIAL`, and no destructive test touched that device. This is recorded in the test report.

## Findings

- **KL-1 (Low, measured)** — `DeviceTrustStore` silently resets to "nothing verified" on key loss, and the next verification rewrites the file with *only* the new record. Fail-closed, silent, total.
- **KL-7 (Low, genuine)** — `AndroidKeystoreCipher` **creates a replacement master key from inside the decrypt path**; afterwards `SecurityInspector` reports "Keystore key present" while every previously stored secret is unreadable. Data-wise fail-closed, reporting-wise wrong. Not fixed — no production change was authorised.
- **KL-2…KL-6 (informational)** — publication-key loss resets the conflict-recovery budget; `wrap()` re-creates a missing alias (full caller inventory: unreachable for identity/database/attachment, lossy-but-safe for trust/publication); the `deviceKey` binding lives one layer above the store; ledger writes inherit the vault lock (all call sites verified inside `withVaultLock`); CRC32 is accident-detection by design.

## Two corrections to earlier claims

- **C-1** — §35's "one builder for 7 production aliases" is **false**: there are two builder sites (`KeystoreAesGcm.ensureKey()` for six, `AndroidKeystoreCipher.secretKey()` for `konnex.master.v1`), with identical parameters but different IV rules and different decrypt behaviour. §35 corrected in place; C-1 recorded in §36.
- **C-2** — "Settings reports the same truth with a missing master key" holds only *before* the first decrypt, which re-creates the alias.

## Evidence actually produced

`core:security` 864 (432 × debug+release, incl. the 6 new tests) · `core:network` 30 · `feature:settings` 80 · `domain:backup` 68 · `server` 175 — all forced (`--rerun-tasks`), sequential, **0 failures/errors/skips**; `ClockGuardMultiProcessTest` 4/4 and `VaultProcessLockTest` 3/3 re-ran real second JVMs. Nine frozen vectors re-reproduced in Python 3.14.7 (`sha256 4ea38ecb…3f81`, `ALL FROZEN VECTORS REPRODUCED`). One new test failed on its first run because it read the store back through the wrapper it had just broken; the assertion was corrected to read with the sealing key and is disclosed in the test report — no assertion was relaxed and no production code was touched.

## 2\. Primary objective — KL-7

Investigate:

`AndroidKeystoreCipher.secretKey()`

The Phase 3.1.22 audit found that the decrypt path can create a replacement `konnex.master.v1` key when the original alias is missing.

This creates misleading recovery behaviour: the new alias exists, but ciphertext encrypted with the old key remains permanently unreadable.

Trace every caller of:

- encrypt
- decrypt
- wrap
- unwrap
- secretKey
- ensureKey

Determine the intended semantics of each operation.

## 3\. Required security behaviour

Separate key acquisition into explicit operations:

- Create key: permitted only during an authorised first-time initialisation or explicit migration.
- Load existing key: must never create a replacement.
- Decrypt: must never silently recreate a missing key.
- Missing key: return a typed, distinguishable failure.
- Invalidated key: preserve the distinction where the platform allows it.
- Recovery: require an explicit, documented recovery procedure.

Do not assume that creating a new key restores access to existing ciphertext.

Do not automatically overwrite existing encrypted data.

1. Missing alias during decrypt.

3. Existing ciphertext with missing master key.

6. Explicit first-time initialisation.
7. Migration behaviour.
8. SecurityInspector reporting.
9. Trust/publication store recovery.
10. Existing database and attachment encryption callers.

Tests must prove that failed decryption does not create a replacement alias.

Do not weaken existing tests.

- Existing encrypted databases.

## What was actually wrong (re-derived from the file, not inherited)

`AndroidKeystoreCipher.secretKey()` was a **getter-or-factory** — it returned an existing entry *or generated one* — and **both** `encryptToBase64` and `decryptFromBase64` called it:

```kotlin
cipher.init(Cipher.DECRYPT_MODE, secretKey(), GCMParameterSpec(TAG_BITS, iv))  // may generate
```

So decrypting data whose alias was gone generated K₂ under `konnex.master.v1`, then failed the GCM tag check. Three harms, only the first of which was recorded before: K₁-sealed data became permanently unreadable; `isKeyAvailable()` (alias presence) flipped true so `SecurityInspector` reported "Keystore key present"; and later writes succeeded, sealing new values under K₂ beside unreadable ones — an inconsistent store self-reporting as healthy.

The other six aliases were already fail-closed on read (`KeystoreAesGcm.wrappingKey()` never creates). The fail-open policy guarded the **most reachable** store — every sign-in and every `deviceId()` call. New finding **KL-8**: two acquisition policies for seven aliases; now unified so the asymmetry cannot return.

## The correction

- New `MasterKeySlot` (`status`/`load`/`create`/`delete`); `AndroidKeystoreSlot` is the only Android impl and the **single** `KeyGenParameterSpec` builder for all seven aliases (C-1's duplication is now gone).
- `existingKey()` probes status then loads — **never creates**. `createKeyIfMissing()` is the only creation entry.
- `MasterKeyInitialisationPolicy`: create only when the alias is missing **and** the store holds no sealed value. `SecureSecretStore.encryptForStorage` is the sole consumer, so a key-loss write fails typed and writes nothing.
- Typed failures: `MasterKeyException.Missing/.Invalidated/.Unavailable` → `KonnexError.KeyUnavailable(alias, reason, cause)`; `domain/backup` maps it to `KEYSTORE_UNAVAILABLE`, not "corrupt container".
- `SecurityReport.masterKeyStatus`; `inspect()` is now side-effect free.

**Limitation worth repeating:** an installation that already lost the master key under the old code now *looks* healthy — a replacement key is present, some entries decrypt, others don't. "Sealed with another key" is not distinguishable from "corrupt envelope" with the current envelope, so no detector was invented and no repair is claimed (L-1). The fix prevents the next occurrence; it does not resurrect K₁.

### Implemented (reachable from the app)
Composition root + Compose navigation; Room v1 with 9 tables behind SQLCipher 4.17.0 (`PRAGMA cipher_version` verified at every open, fail-closed on plaintext/ambiguous/wrong-key states); Keystore-wrapped DB key and AES-GCM secret store; OTP sign-in with tokens kept only in the secret store; session derived from persisted state; transactional message write + preview + outbox enqueue; tombstones; full sync engine (push outbox, paginated pull, monotonic cursor); sync triggers for startup, foreground, connectivity return and 15-min WorkManager; proactive serialized token refresh with single-use rotation; encrypted attachments and `KONNXBK2` encrypted backups; WebDAV provider with real `MKCOL/PUT/GET/DELETE/PROPFIND`.

### Tested on JVM (this session)
`:sync:test` 18, `:domain:sync:test` 13, `:domain:messaging:test` 4 — 35 tests, 0 failures.

### Built successfully
`:app:assembleDebug --rerun-tasks` → BUILD SUCCESSFUL (2 m 09 s), `app-debug.apk` produced.

### Verified on Android
**Nothing in this phase.** Last artifact on disk is a single instrumentation shard (6 tests, 0 failures); `README` claims 73/73 on API 28 and API 35 refer to an earlier milestone and do not cover today's 28 classes / 195 `@Test` methods.

### Not yet implemented
Push (no FCM, no service, no `POST_NOTIFICATIONS` — background latency is up to 15 min); attachments in the composer (`feature/conversations` has no picker, though storage/encryption/upload primitives exist); Google Drive and remote-server storage providers; E2EE message encryption (server can read bodies); calling media/Telecom (`capabilities` are hard-coded `false`); Room schema migrations (v1 is the only version).

### Blocked / claim limitations
Device and emulator commands are prohibited, so runtime verification and Keystore/multiprocess evidence for this tree are simply **unrecorded** — reported as not verified, never as passing. Independent crypto review (F11-5) remains external.

### Three findings that change the plan
1. **Conflict policy is tested but not wired.** `SyncMergePolicy`/`SyncPlanner.plan` implement five decisions including `REQUEUE_LOCAL`; `SyncEngineImpl` calls only `nextCursor` and uses a narrower inline revision guard. So the "no silent data loss" branch never executes — the one real Phase 2 correctness gap.
2. **`app-debug.apk` is 322 MB** (~287 MB is four unstripped `libsignal_jni.so` ABIs, no `abiFilters`). Cheapest fix in the backlog; it directly slows every verification cycle.
3. **Phase 2 is ~85 % already done** — the design document is a completion plan (wire the merge policy, contact push, outbox observability, push trigger seam), not a greenfield one.

### One thing you should know

### Bugs fixed
1. **Real packaging defect:** 287 MB of the 307 MB debug APK was four copies of one unstripped native library, with no `abiFilters`. Debug now ships `arm64-v8a` + `x86_64` only — measured −140.8 MB, install time drops from minutes to seconds on the LG-G710, SM-S134DL and the API 35 x86_64 AVD. Release keeps all ABIs; `minSdk` is 26, so a 32-bit test phone would need the 6-line block removed (documented).
2. **Documentation defect:** `publish()` claimed the atomic move prevents overwriting another writer's key file. On Android `ATOMIC_MOVE` is `rename(2)` and replaces; safety actually comes from the OS file lock. Comment corrected, no behaviour changed.

**No functional defect was found** in the Room, SQLCipher, Keystore, migration, outbox or recovery code. Two non-defects recorded instead of silently "fixed": the dead `LEGACY_PLAINTEXT` enum constant, and `DatabasePersistenceTest` asserting persistence but not the "never regresses" half of its own name.

### What remains
Execute 195 tests in four batches, run the two restart classes **twice** with `am force-stop` between runs (the only proof of decrypt-after-restart — a single green run silently skips them via `assumeTrue`), run the multi-process kill class with adb orchestration, and decide whether `DeviceSyncTest` is in scope (needs `-e phone`, a running server, two accounts).

### Which tests need an API 35 emulator
**186 of 195** on the emulator alone: 172 in batches 1–4, plus 8 restart tests (run twice), plus 6 kill tests. The remaining 9 (`DeviceSyncTest`) additionally need a server + `-e phone`. Hardware-backed StrongBox and real two-phone delivery cannot be proven on an emulator at all.

# OBJECTIVE

Start Stage 2 with the most important existing correctness issue.

Previous audit identified that:

- `SyncMergePolicy` and `SyncPlanner.plan` already implement conflict-resolution decisions.
- These components are unit-tested.
- `SyncEngineImpl` does not actually use the planner.
- Its narrower inline revision check may overwrite unsynchronized local changes.

Independently verify this finding before changing code.

# TASK 1 – FRESH AUDIT

- Inspect Git status and HEAD.
- Preserve all existing modifications and untracked files.
- Inspect the current synchronization implementation.
- Inspect Room entities and DAO transaction boundaries.
- Inspect outbox, cursor and revision handling.
- Inspect token refresh and rotation.
- Inspect existing synchronization tests.

Do not trust previous reports without source verification.

# TASK 2 – WIRE THE CONFLICT-RESOLUTION POLICY

Integrate the existing `SyncPlanner` and `SyncMergePolicy` into the real `SyncEngineImpl` execution path.

Requirements:

- Preserve unsynchronized local edits.
- Never silently discard pending outbox operations.
- Handle remote updates and local revisions deterministically.
- Handle duplicate and out-of-order server events.
- Preserve monotonic cursor progression.
- Handle tombstones and deleted messages correctly.
- Ensure database updates and cursor advancement are transactionally consistent.
- Preserve retryability after partial failures.
- Avoid duplicate message insertion.
- Maintain backward compatibility with existing server APIs.

Do not create a second conflict-resolution implementation if the existing planner can be reused.

# TASK 3 – ADVERSARIAL TESTING

Add tests for:

- Local edit versus remote edit.
- Pending outbox versus remote deletion.
- Duplicate remote events.
- Out-of-order revisions.
- Cursor advancement after transaction rollback.
- Network failure during synchronization.
- Process restart with pending operations.
- Repeated sync idempotency.
- Concurrent sync requests.

Use deterministic tests and the existing project test infrastructure.

# TASK 4 – AUTOMATIC SYNC TRIGGERS

Audit the existing triggers:

- Application startup.
- Returning to foreground.
- Network connectivity restoration.
- WorkManager periodic fallback.

Verify that triggers do not create overlapping synchronization jobs or cause unnecessary network requests.

Only fix demonstrated defects.

# TASK 5 – TOKEN REFRESH AND ROTATION

Verify:

- Expired access token handling.
- Proactive refresh.
- Concurrent refresh serialization.
- Single-use refresh-token rotation.
- Old-token rejection.
- Retry after temporary network failure.
- Behaviour after refresh-token reuse or revocation.

Preserve existing authentication contracts.

**Stage 2.1 is complete.** The roadmap's six stages are untouched, and I stopped before Stage 3.

## What was actually wrong (re-verified in source, not trusted from the old report)

1. `SyncEngineImpl` used `SyncPlanner.nextCursor` only — the five-way merge policy was dead code.
2. `applyMessage` overwrote the remote body unconditionally for any newer revision, including a message with an unsent local edit.
3. `applyConversation` / `applyContact` had **no** revision guard at all — stale or replayed events could rewind them and add unread counts again.
4. Tombstones deleted rows without touching the outbox, so a queued send could resurrect a deleted message.
5. The cursor advanced in a separate transaction from the rows it described; a failing cursor write escaped `syncNow` as an exception.
6. The coordinator is single-flight, but `SyncWorker` calls the engine directly — two runs could push the same batch.

## What changed

- `SyncEngineImpl.applyPage()` now calls `SyncPlanner.plan` per page inside the same transaction as the cursor update, and executes `APPLY_REMOTE` / `KEEP_LOCAL` / `REQUEUE_LOCAL` / `ALREADY_IN_SYNC`. No revision comparison remains in the engine's decision path.
- `REQUEUE_LOCAL` keeps the user's text, adopts the remote base revision and restores the attempt budget (`adoptRemoteBase` + `requeueOutbox`). A delete supersedes a queued send and is logged with a row count, not silently.
- `SyncMergePolicy` gained an explicit tombstone rule (delete wins over a pending edit; an older tombstone still loses) — added in the planner, not the engine.
- Page + cursor commit together; a failure rolls both back and is returned as `KonnexError.Storage` instead of escaping.
- `runLock` in `syncNow()` serialises runs from every trigger owner.
- `core/database` changes are **queries only** — no entity/schema change, no migration.

Token rotation itself is already correct: 7 server cases + 4 client cases pass, and no auth file was touched.
